# Noctívago — archived development history

This file is **not** auto-loaded into Claude Code sessions. It holds the older entries from `CLAUDE.md`’s running "Since the above was written" log, moved here verbatim (nothing summarized or deleted) to keep the live `CLAUDE.md` lean. See `CLAUDE.md` for current architecture and recent releases; see the repo-root `CHANGELOG.md` for the user-facing release notes.

Entries below span roughly v0.1.26 through the v0.1.145 self-review pass - the Export plugin, the Composite plugin, the Preset Remix plugin's own superseded writeup, and everything shipped between them are now here too (most recently archived 2026-09-06). Later history (the merged Remix tab, v0.1.146 onward) stays in `CLAUDE.md`.

---

Since the above was written: Remix gained Speed/Pitch/Reverse controls (ffmpeg `rubberband`/`areverse`, Speed also live on streamed sounds via `playbackRate`), a two-pass bake ordering fix so those don't reintroduce a loop seam, faster/reversible auto-zoom while dragging a trim handle, and — per explicit owner prioritization over the rest of the backlog — the Loop vs. Random Interval ("scatter") playback mode described above.

**The parametric EQ was substantially deepened after direct research into FL Studio's "Fruity Parametric EQ 2"** (the app's own stated reference, not an assumption — WebSearch + the official Image-Line manual), triggered by the user repeatedly asking "do you actually know how this works?" rather than letting scope drift on assumption. What shipped from that research: **7 bands** (was 6; `defaultEqBands()` in both `library.js` and `plugins/editor/index.js`), each independently one of **8 filter types** via a `type` field (`off/lowpass/highpass/bandpass/notch/lowshelf/highshelf/peaking`, a `<select>` next to the Freq/Gain/Q fields) — Web Audio's native `BiquadFilterNode.type` covers 7 of these directly; `'off'` maps to `'allpass'` at the node level (a true no-op on the amplitude spectrum) via an `eqNodeType(type)` helper duplicated in `SoundSource.js` and `PreviewSource.js`. Baking dispatches per band to the matching real ffmpeg filter (`loopClip.js`'s `buildEqBandFilter`: `lowpass`/`highpass`/`bandpass`/`bandreject`/`bass`/`treble`/`equalizer`, all verified chained together against the real bundled binary first). A **real-time spectrum analyzer** replaced the static band-energy backdrop as the graph's primary visualization: `PreviewSource.js` taps an `AnalyserNode` off `outputGainNode` (the point both crossfade voices combine into), and `EqEditor.js`'s `tickSpectrum()` reads `getByteFrequencyData()` once per frame from the *same* rAF loop that already drives the playhead — drawn as a pink fill (`rgba(247,118,142,...)`, matching the reference) through the graph's own log-frequency mapping. The static backdrop (`bandEnergy.js`) wasn't removed — it's now the fallback shown via `drawBandEnergyBackdrop()` whenever nothing is playing (`clearSpectrum()` on pause/stop). Deliberately *not* cloned, with reasoning tied to Noctívago's own signal architecture rather than just scope-cutting: Mid/Side/L/R monitoring, Linear Phase (FFT) mode, phase rotation display, and the HQ+/HQL/HQE quality tiers all exist to manage phase/latency problems that only arise when a filtered signal is later recombined with a correlated copy of itself (parallel processing, live DAW monitoring) — Noctívago's sounds each play back independently and are never recombined with anything correlated to themselves, so these simply don't apply here. **A/B Compare** was kept in scope (initially miscategorized with the dropped DAW-specific features, then reconsidered) since it's generic workflow utility, not mixing-console-specific: two buttons (`editor-eq-compare-a`/`-b`) next to Reset EQ snapshot the live 7-band config into an in-memory slot (`this.eqCompare.slots.A`/`.B`, `switchEqCompareSlot()`) and toggle between them — the first switch into an untouched slot seeds it as a copy of the current bands (so it doesn't jar into something different), each slot then diverges independently as the user edits. Per-editing-session only (reset on every `loadSound()`, not persisted) — a comparison aid, not a saved setting. Also shipped in this stretch: **double-click a node to reset just that band** to its own default (`EqEditor.js`'s `onDoubleClick`, mirrors the app-wide slider-reset convention for these canvas-drawn nodes it can't otherwise reach), **Alt+click as a faster alternative to double-click for the same reset** (checked in `pointerDown` before starting a drag, fires the identical `onDoubleClick` callback since both are just different triggers for the same reset action), **Ctrl+click fine-tune drag** (holding Ctrl while dragging a node shrinks mouse movement into parameter movement by `FINE_TUNE_SCALE` (0.2), applied to the per-frame delta rather than re-deriving an absolute position from the cursor every frame — so toggling Ctrl mid-drag never causes a jump, only the scale of movement going forward changes; verified via CDP by comparing the gain delta from an identical raw mouse movement with and without Ctrl held, and by toggling Ctrl mid-drag and confirming a smooth, proportional transition), **per-band Mute/Solo** (two buttons next to the Freq/Gain/Q fields, scoped to the currently-selected band like those fields already are. Mute is a real, persisted `band.muted` boolean — non-destructive on purpose, unlike switching a band's `type` to `'off'`, it never touches freq/gain/Q/type, so re-enabling a band restores exactly what it was tuned to; `eqNodeType(band)` in both `SoundSource.js` and `PreviewSource.js` now takes the whole band object and bypasses to `'allpass'` on `band.muted || band.type === 'off'`, and `loopClip.js`'s `buildEqBandFilter` skips a muted band entirely from the baked ffmpeg chain. Solo is the opposite: preview-only, per-editing-session state that lives on the `EqEditor.js` controller (`soloIndex`, reset on every `load()`) and is deliberately never part of the saved `filters.eq` — `index.js`'s `previewFilters()` builds an audition-only copy of the current filters (every band except the soloed one forced to `muted: true`, the soloed band force-unmuted so soloing overrides its own real mute) and that's what actually gets sent to `previewSource.setFilters()`, while `currentFilters()` — the real, saved state — stays untouched; bypassed nodes also render dimmed on the EQ graph itself so it's obvious at a glance which bands are actually contributing. Verified via CDP: toggling Mute correctly flagged the sound as having unsaved changes and bypassed the live `BiquadFilterNode` to `allpass`; toggling Solo did neither of the first two (no dirty flag, since it's not part of what saves) but did correctly bypass every *other* band's live node while leaving a separately-muted band's real `.muted` flag untouched underneath), and the **dev-only window-positioning fix** — `src/main/index.js`'s `devWindowPosition()` now computes the target display's centered coordinates *before* `BrowserWindow` construction (passed straight into the constructor) instead of moving the window after launch, eliminating a visible flash on the primary display that a post-hoc move couldn't avoid.

**The Mixer's sound list auto-groups currently-playing sounds to the top, loudest first** (`src/renderer/ui/SoundList.js`'s `splitNowPlaying`) — restores behavior the owner wanted back ("what's actually making noise right now" at a glance), framed as an automatic group rather than a manual toggle so there's nothing to remember to turn on. Only kicks in with no explicit `groupMode` chosen (defers to an explicit alphabetical/date/tags grouping exactly like drag-reorder already does) and only when something's actually playing — an idle mixer, or one with an explicit group mode selected, renders exactly as before. "Loudest" is each sound's own per-sound volume (`state.volumes`, 0-1) — global/master volume applies uniformly so it can't affect *relative* loudness ranking. Ties fall back to whatever order the chosen `sortMode` already produced (name/date/custom), since `Array.prototype.sort` is stable. Drag-reorder (`custom` sort mode) is disabled while this auto-split is active, same reasoning as the existing grouped-mode guard: a row could land in a different section on the very next render (e.g. it stops playing), which would make "which row did you actually drag" ambiguous.

**BUG FIX: the Remix EQ's live spectrum analyzer shrank/faded whenever the preview volume slider was turned down**, reported directly by the user ("it's just a control of volume for the tab and shouldn't tamper with tools for editing the audio") — correct: `PreviewSource.js`'s `analyserNode` used to tap `outputGainNode` directly, which carries both the preview volume slider's own level *and* the play/pause fade-in/out ramp (both animate `outputGainNode.gain`), so the spectrum display was reflecting listening level, not the actual filtered signal. Fixed by adding a second, parallel connection from both crossfade voices into a dedicated `analyserFeedNode` (a plain `GainNode` fixed at `1`, never touched by `setVolume`/`play`/`pause`) and tapping the analyser from that instead — still correctly reflects whichever voice is audible (crossfade state) and any EQ/filter changes (both happen upstream, in each voice's own filter chain), just decoupled from the volume slider. Verified via CDP: sampled the analyser's real `getByteFrequencyData()` output at full preview volume, then again after dropping `setVolume(0.05)` (confirmed via `outputGainNode.gain.value` reading `0.05`) — the analyser's byte-sum stayed within normal frame-to-frame variance (806 → 893) instead of collapsing proportionally the way it did with the old tap.

**A sound name truncated by window width now marquee-scrolls on hover** (`SoundRow.js`'s `wireMarquee`) to reveal the full text — `.sound-row-name` is already `white-space:nowrap` + `overflow:hidden` + `text-overflow:ellipsis`, so `scrollWidth > clientWidth` is exactly "this name is truncated," nothing extra to check. On `mouseenter`, computes the overflow distance and a pixels-per-second pace that scales up mildly with how much text needs to scroll (a genuinely long name reads a bit brisker, not just proportionally longer, capped at `MARQUEE_MAX_PX_PER_SEC`), sets `--marquee-distance`/`--marquee-duration` custom properties, and toggles a class that drives a CSS `@keyframes` animation (translateX to the negative overflow distance and back, held briefly at both ends) — `mouseleave` removes the class, which resets the transform cleanly since the animation was its only source. Verified via CDP: renamed a test sound to a name overflowing its row, confirmed real `scrollWidth`/`clientWidth` measurements agreed there was truncation, dispatched a synthetic `mouseenter` and confirmed the class/custom-properties applied and a mid-animation screenshot showed the text genuinely scrolled off to one side; confirmed a short, non-truncated name's `mouseenter` never adds the class at all (an exact `scrollWidth === clientWidth` no-op); confirmed `mouseleave` fully resets `transform` to `none`.

**App-wide number-box stepper** (`src/renderer/core/numberBoxStepper.js`, installed once in `main.js` alongside `sliderReset.js`, same delegated-listener pattern — any current or future `<input type="number">`, core or any plugin's, gets it for free): hover a number field and scroll, or use Arrow Up/Down while it's focused, to step its value — plain ±1, Ctrl ±0.1 (finer), Shift jumps to the next/previous multiple of 10, Alt to the next/previous multiple of 100 (exact rules given directly by the user, with worked examples: 3 + Shift → 10, 13 + Alt → 100). Clamped to the input's own `min`/`max` attributes when present. **Wheel is deliberately gated on the input already being focused, not just hovered** — several panels (Remix especially) are themselves mouse-wheel-scrollable and now have many number inputs in them (EQ Freq/Gain/Q, scatter gap min/max); reacting to a bare hover-and-scroll-past while the user is just trying to scroll the page would turn every one of those fields into an accidental value-nudging trap. Requiring a click first matches how every other scroll-to-adjust control already works and avoids that footgun entirely — arrow keys need no equivalent guard, since a keydown event only ever targets whatever's already focused. Verified via CDP against the real EQ Freq/Gain/Q fields: every modifier combination (plain/Ctrl/Shift/Alt, both directions) for both wheel and arrow-key input produced the exact expected value, including the user's own two worked examples; confirmed a value clamps correctly against a field's real `min`/`max` (Alt from 13 on Gain, whose max is 24, landed at 24, not the unclamped 100); confirmed a wheel event dispatched at an *unfocused* input while a different one has focus is a complete no-op (value unchanged, `preventDefault()` never called) — proving normal page/panel scrolling stays untouched.

**A floating "scroll to top" button in Remix** (`wireScrollTop()`) — requested directly since the panel's grown long (waveform, times, playmode, crossfade/scatter, speed/pitch, filters, 7-band EQ, presets). `#tab-content` is the *shared* scroll container every tab's panel lives inside (`main { overflow-y: auto }` in `main.css`) — only the active tab's own `<section>` is ever visible at a time (`TabHost.js` toggles a hidden class on the rest), so appending the button inside Remix's own panel markup means it automatically disappears whenever a different tab is active, the same way the rest of the tab's content already does — no extra `onShow`/`onHide` bookkeeping needed just for that (confirmed via CDP: the button's own `display` computed style stays unchanged switching tabs, but its bounding rect collapses to 0×0 since its hidden ancestor generates no boxes at all). Shows itself past a 300px scroll threshold, scrolls smoothly to top on click and re-hides; `onShow` re-syncs visibility against whatever the shared container's `scrollTop` happens to be (leftover from whichever tab was previously active, not necessarily meaningful for Remix's own layout).

**Play/Pause converted from text to icons** (Mixer's global toolbar button, Remix's sticky preview button) — the first item from a broader icon-conversion pass, requested directly, with the user explicitly wanting to be asked before anything ambiguous gets icon-ified (via a `## Questions` section in the Obsidian inbox, see [[feedback_questions_via_obsidian_inbox]]). Play/Pause itself was pre-cleared as unambiguous (a near-universal triangle/two-bars pair). A shared inline SVG pair (`PLAY_ICON_SVG`/`PAUSE_ICON_SVG`, duplicated between `tabs/mixer/index.js` and `plugins/editor/index.js` for the usual cross-directory reason) swaps via `innerHTML`, with a `title` attribute keeping the actual wording ("Play all"/"Pause all" vs. plain "Play"/"Pause") available on hover so the Mixer's "all" scope isn't lost just because the glyph doesn't say it. New `.btn-icon` utility class (fixed 34×34px, centered, zero padding) for icon-only buttons going forward. **User's answer on the broader pass**: "If you feel like it's gonna be confusing use icon+text" — for anything in the more ambiguous bucket, pair icon+text rather than dropping the label; that pass (plus a new, unprompted ask in the same reply — a mute/unmute icon beside every volume slider) is still in progress, not done in this same change.

**A mute/unmute icon button now sits beside every volume slider app-wide** (Mixer's global volume, each sound's own per-sound volume, Remix's preview volume) — a speaker/speaker-with-X SVG pair (duplicated per module/plugin, matching the Play/Pause pair's own duplication pattern) that toggles on click and visually reacts (`.btn-svg-icon-active`, a red border/icon) to the current mute state. **Mute is layered on top of the volume itself, never destructive**: muting forces the actual live audio gain to 0 without touching the slider's own tracked value (`state.volumes`/`state.globalMuted`+`engine.masterGain`/Remix's `previewMuted`+`previewVolume`), so unmuting always restores exactly wherever the slider was left. Dragging a volume slider while muted implicitly unmutes, matching how most OS/media-player volume controls already behave — for per-sound sliders specifically, this is the one branch of `changeVolume()` that still triggers a `render()` (the common per-tick case stays render()-free, so a drag never reorders/redraws the list mid-drag; only the rare "was muted, now dragging" transition needs the row's icon to actually update). **Named the new icon-only-button utility class `.btn-svg-icon`, not `.btn-icon`** — a real naming collision was caught before shipping: `.btn-icon` already existed for the gear/+ *glyph* buttons (sized via padding/font-size, no fixed box), and reusing that name for a real inline-SVG button (which needs a fixed square box and zero padding to center cleanly) would have silently broken both button families' sizing via cascade order, not a visible crash — caught by re-reading the stylesheet after adding the new rule, not by the (still visually plausible-looking) screenshot alone. Verified via CDP against the real dev app: read a live source's actual `.volume` property directly (not just the slider's displayed value) before/after muting a playing sound (0.95 → 0 → 0.95 restored exactly on unmute); toggled global mute and confirmed the flag flipped; dragged a muted sound's slider and confirmed it auto-unmuted with the new value applied; confirmed the Remix sticky mute button's title/active-class updated correctly on click.

**Pressing Enter in the Remix sound picker now defaults to the first result** when nothing's been arrow-navigated yet (`options[this.pickerHighlightIndex] ?? options[0]`, was previously `options[this.pickerHighlightIndex]` alone — `-1` indexes nowhere, so Enter silently did nothing until an explicit arrow-down) — requested directly: typing a precise search query and hitting Enter shouldn't need an extra arrow-down first, since a precise query usually narrows straight down to the one sound meant. Arrow-navigating still selects whatever's explicitly highlighted, not just the first result. The Mixer's own search bar has no equivalent gap — it's a pure live-filter over the full list with no single "selected" concept the way a dropdown picker has, so there was nothing to fix there.

**Settings gained a configurable auto-update check interval** — a text field (placeholder exactly `hh:mm:ss`, per the request) replacing the previously-hardcoded 4-hour periodic re-check. `settings.js` gained `autoUpdateIntervalSeconds` (still defaulting to 4 hours) alongside a new `setAutoUpdateIntervalSeconds(seconds)` that clamps to a 60-second floor so a mistyped near-zero value can't hammer the update endpoint. `autoUpdate.js`'s `startPeriodicChecks()` now reads that setting instead of a fixed constant, and its own exported `setAutoUpdateIntervalSeconds` restarts the running timer immediately against the new value (rather than waiting for the stale interval to elapse once more first) whenever a check is already scheduled. The field itself commits on blur/Enter (matching the sound-rename field's own pattern elsewhere), reformats to a canonical zero-padded `hh:mm:ss` on success, and reverts to whatever's still actually persisted on an unparseable or non-positive entry — reuses `util/time.js`'s existing `parseDuration` (already handles h:m:s) rather than writing a second parser, with a small local `formatHms` for display since the existing `formatDuration` omits the hour segment when it's zero, which wouldn't match this field's own always-three-segments placeholder. Verified via CDP: confirmed the default field value; typed a lenient shorthand (`1:30:0`) and confirmed it parsed to 5400 seconds and reformatted to `01:30:00`; typed garbage and confirmed the field reverted to the last actually-persisted value without touching it; typed a 30-second value and confirmed the backend clamp brought it up to the 60-second floor and the field reflected the real clamped result, not the raw input.

**Icon+text pass for the ambiguous button bucket** — closes out the whole icon-conversion arc from this batch. Per the user's own answer ("If you feel like it's gonna be confusing use icon+text"), every remaining button from the original survey's middle/ambiguous group got a small SVG icon *paired with* its existing label, never replacing it — Presets (bookmark), the dialog Cancel buttons (×), the dialog Add/confirm buttons (check), Save current mix (floppy disk), Remix's Save/Reset EQ/Reset filters (floppy disk / undo-arrow), Relink (chain-link), and preset Load/Delete plus the Mixer's per-sound Remove (folder-open / trash) — a slight scope broadening from icon-only to icon+text for consistency, applied uniformly across this whole pass rather than mixing bare-icon and icon+text styles for action buttons. New `.btn-icon-text` utility class (`display:inline-flex; align-items:center; gap:6px`), clearly distinct in purpose from `.btn-svg-icon`: icon+text is for *action* buttons with real textual nuance worth keeping, `.btn-svg-icon` stays reserved for binary *toggle* controls (Play/Pause, Mute) where the icon alone is already unambiguous and space is tight. Remix's Save button (3 dynamic text states: Save/Saving…/Nothing to save) needed a small structural change to keep the icon from getting wiped every re-render — the changeable text now lives in its own `<span id="editor-save-label">` child, with both `.textContent`-setting call sites retargeted at the span instead of the button itself, so the icon persists as a permanent sibling. **One real layout regression caught and fixed during verification**: the Presets modal's "Save current mix" button initially wrapped its icon+label onto two lines (not enough room in its input+button row) — traced to the button's default flex-shrink still letting the row's other item claim space it needed; fixed with a `#save-preset`-scoped `flex-shrink: 0; white-space: nowrap` rather than a blanket rule on `.btn-icon-text` (a first attempt at a blanket fix was tried and reverted — it broke Remix's *own* Save button in the opposite direction, whose "Nothing to save" text legitimately needs room to shrink in a tighter row). **Also noted, not fixed (pre-existing, out of scope for this pass)**: the dev window carries a small (~54-60px) horizontal scroll overflow somewhere in the Remix panel's layout, visible in CDP screenshots throughout this whole session predating this change — confirmed via direct measurement (`document.body.scrollWidth` vs `window.innerWidth`) that new buttons render fully and correctly once scrolled into view, so this is a separate, pre-existing minor issue, not a regression from this pass.

**BUG FIX: the toolbar was overflowing the window's right edge (~54px, clipped invisibly off-screen)** — this was the "pre-existing minor horizontal scroll overflow" noted (but not chased down) during the icon+text pass; turned out not to be pre-existing or Remix-specific at all. Root cause, found by walking every element's `getBoundingClientRect()` and flagging anything whose right edge exceeded `window.innerWidth`: the toolbar's own `<button id="add-sound">` ("+") and `<button id="open-settings">` (gear) were the actual offenders, pushed past the visible edge by the toolbar's total content width exceeding the default 480px window — a direct consequence of this session's own additions (the new global mute button, and Presets growing from plain text to icon+text, together added roughly 80px). Fixed with `flex-wrap: wrap` on `.toolbar` (`main.css`) — the overflow items now drop to a clean second row instead of clipping invisibly off-screen, which is strictly better regardless of exactly how tight the toolbar gets at any given window width. Verified via CDP: `document.body.scrollWidth` matched `window.innerWidth` exactly after the fix (was 518 vs. 464 before), and a screenshot confirmed the Settings/+ buttons wrap to a tidy second row rather than disappearing off-screen.

**BUG FIX: adjusting the EQ's Freq/Gain/Q number fields via hover+scroll or arrow keys silently did nothing** — reported directly ("raising the frequency by the number doesn't change the node's position and doesn't affect the audio"). Root cause: a real regression from this session's own app-wide number-box stepper (`numberBoxStepper.js`, `v0.1.53`), which dispatches a synthetic `'input'` event on scroll/arrow-key adjustment - but the EQ's Freq/Gain/Q fields were the one place in the whole app still wired to `'change'` (fires only on blur/Enter) instead of `'input'` (fires live, matching every other numeric field: Scatter gap min/max, the core filter sliders). The stepper's `'input'` dispatch simply never reached `applyEqFieldsToSelectedBand()`, so scroll/arrow-key edits updated the field's own displayed value but never touched the actual band, the node's position on the graph, or the live audio. Fixed by switching `eqFreq`/`eqGain`/`eqQ` to `'input'` listeners (the Type `<select>` correctly stays on `'change'`, the only sensible event for a dropdown). Verified via CDP: dispatched a synthetic `input` event on the Freq field (matching the stepper's real trigger), confirmed the band data, the graph controller's own state, and the live `BiquadFilterNode.frequency` (after letting its `setTargetAtTime` smoothing ramp settle) all agreed at the new value; separately confirmed manual typing + blur still works correctly, ruling out a regression from the fix itself.

**The parametric EQ was reworked from a fixed 7-band set into a fully user-managed, dynamic node system** — reversing an earlier deliberate scope cut, after direct feedback that the fixed set felt "too crowded" ("It should be blank with nodes and you would click a + button to add a node and a trash can button to delete a selected node/drag the node into the trash can button to delete it too. This should take precedence over anything"). `defaultEqBands()` (in both `library.js` and `plugins/editor/index.js`) now returns `[]` — a fresh or reset EQ starts genuinely blank, with a "Click + to add a band" hint drawn directly on the canvas. `EqEditor.js` gained `addBand()` (pushes a neutral peaking band at 1000Hz, selects it) and `removeSelectedBand()` (deletes whichever band is selected — the one shared path for both the trash button's click and dragging a node onto it, hit-tested via a registered `setTrashDropTarget(el)` element in `pointerUp`), plus careful `soloIndex` bookkeeping across deletion (cleared if the soloed band was removed, decremented if it pointed past the removed index — since `soloIndex` is a raw array index into a now-mutable-length array).

**This forced a real architectural change to the live audio chain**, not just the UI: `PreviewSource.js`'s per-voice `eqNodes` and `SoundSource.js`'s equivalent used to be built *once*, at construction, assuming a fixed band count forever. Both gained a `_rebuildEqChain`/`rebuildEqChain` method that tears down and reconnects the chain (`lowpassNode → eqNodes[0..N] → filterGainNode`, or `lowpassNode → filterGainNode` directly when N=0) whenever `setFilters()` sees the band count actually change — `.disconnect()` with no arguments removes every outgoing connection from a node, so this is safe regardless of the old or new count. An unchanged band count still takes the cheap in-place `setTargetAtTime` path, same as before. Verified via CDP against both audio paths independently: `PreviewSource`'s live voice correctly rebuilt from 3 nodes down to 2 after a drag-to-trash deletion; `SoundSource`'s core Mixer playback correctly rebuilt from 6 real saved bands down to 1, and separately down to 0 (confirmed playback stayed unpaused throughout, matching the always-additive "buffer mode only affects seam smoothness" ethos this app already has for the rest of the filter chain) — and a sound saved with the old 7-band data still loads and plays correctly today, no migration needed (only the *default* changed, not the schema).

**Double-click and Alt+click, previously identical (both fired a full reset), were split apart** after a direct follow-up: "double clicking shouldn't reset the node completely just position. Alt clicking should be the full reset." With no more canonical per-index default to reset *back to* (bands are user-added now), both were redefined: double-click resets just `gainDb` to 0 (the node's vertical "position"); Alt+click is the fuller reset (`gainDb:0, q:1, type:'peaking'`) while still preserving the node's own frequency — its horizontal identity, not something a reset should relocate. Verified via CDP: a distinctive band (freq 3000, gain 10, Q 4, type peaking) double-clicked came back with only gain zeroed, freq/Q/type untouched; the same setup with type `highshelf` Alt+clicked came back fully neutralized except frequency.

**The EQ graph's connecting line is now the real composite frequency-response curve**, not a straight polyline through node points — also requested directly, twice, with a clarifying follow-up ("the line should start at the left edge of the box... and end at the right edge... when the nodes affect it"). Rather than hand-deriving the RBJ cookbook biquad transfer-function math (real risk of a subtle formula bug), `EqEditor.js` creates a real (never-rendered) `BiquadFilterNode` per active band on a dedicated lazy `OfflineAudioContext` and reads its own native `getFrequencyResponse()` — guaranteed to match what the browser's actual audio graph will do, since it *is* the same implementation. Each active band's magnitude response (one sample per horizontal pixel, spanning the full 20Hz–20kHz width) is summed in dB across bands — correct because the real chain connects bands in series, and a series chain's combined transfer function is the product of each stage's own response (log-domain sum). Bypassed bands (muted, or a different band is soloed) and `'off'`-type bands are excluded, matching the same "honest reflection of what's audible" the existing node-dimming already does. **Performance**: computing this on every single `redraw()` call would be wasteful, since `tickSpectrum()` alone already triggers one every animation frame during playback purely to refresh the live spectrum overlay, with the bands themselves usually unchanged frame to frame — the controller caches the last computed curve, keyed on a cheap `JSON.stringify(bands)` fingerprint plus `soloIndex` and canvas width, and only recomputes when that key actually changes. Verified visually via screenshots: a peaking band showed a real bell curve with the correct Q-shaped width; a highpass band showed a genuine rolloff (flat above the cutoff, dropping to the graph's floor below it) — not a straight line to a point.

**A "Current Mix" group now sits between Now Playing and Everything Else** in the Mixer's sound list (`SoundList.js`'s `splitNowPlaying`) — requested directly via the Obsidian inbox: "Now playing > Current Mix > Everything Else." Extends the existing auto-grouping (see the "loudest-first Now Playing" entry above) into a three-way split: actually-playing sounds first (unchanged), then sounds that are `included` (checked into the mix) but not currently audible — the case a global pause produces, since `included` deliberately stays true through a pause (`toggleIncluded`'s own comment: "the button reflects `included`, not `playing`") — then everything not in the mix at all. Same activation rule as before (only kicks in with no explicit `groupMode` chosen, and now also activates whenever anything is *included*, not just when something's *playing*, since a fully-paused-but-loaded mix should still show its Current Mix tier). Verified via CDP: included two real sounds (both landed in Now Playing while playing), clicked global pause (both correctly moved to Current Mix, header count dropped to two groups), then re-added one individually (correctly split across all three tiers simultaneously — Now Playing/Current Mix/Everything Else all present and correctly populated in one render).

**Number-field hover+scroll no longer requires a click first** (`numberBoxStepper.js`) — a direct, explicit reversal of the focus-gated design shipped in `v0.1.53`: "Just hovering a text box with a number should be grounds for scrolling with mouse wheel and changing it. Not selecting then hovering." The original focus gate existed specifically to stop panels with many number fields (Remix's EQ Freq/Gain/Q, scatter gap min/max) from turning into an accidental-value-nudge trap while scroll-wheeling past them — a real tradeoff, but the owner weighed it and wants hover-only regardless. Implementation is a one-line change: the wheel listener now keys off `evt.target` (whatever's actually under the cursor) instead of `document.activeElement`, so a bare hover-and-scroll now steps the value with no click needed; arrow-key stepping is unaffected (keydown already only ever targets whatever's focused). Verified via CDP: dispatched a synthetic wheel event at a completely unfocused EQ Freq field (`document.activeElement` confirmed empty throughout) and confirmed the value stepped and the live band/audio updated exactly as a focused edit already did.

**Dev-mode audio now defaults to the owner's Yeti mic's monitor-out channel instead of the PC speakers** — requested directly and urgently mid-session ("it's making noise on my home while people sleep and i set my PC to play on the speakers"), after the very next dev relaunch's resume-on-launch feature did exactly that through the real speakers. New `src/renderer/core/devAudioOutput.js` (duplicated into `plugins/editor/audio/` for the Remix plugin's own separate `AudioContext`, same cross-directory-import reason as its other duplicated files) uses the Output Devices API (`AudioContext.prototype.setSinkId`, supported in this Electron/Chromium build) to route to whichever `navigator.mediaDevices.enumerateDevices()` output device's *label* matches `/yeti/i` — a Blue Yeti's built-in headphone-monitor jack shows up as a genuine Windows playback device ("Alto-falantes (Yeti Stereo Microphone)" on this machine). Matches by label rather than a hardcoded `deviceId` since an id is opaque and can drift across driver reinstalls. Both `AudioEngine.js` constructors (core Mixer, Remix plugin) call it fire-and-forget right after building their `AudioContext`; gated on `!app.isPackaged` (via the existing `window.noctivago.isPackaged()` IPC call) so it never runs in a real install, and fully best-effort — any failure (device unplugged, API unsupported, enumeration denied) silently leaves output on the system default rather than breaking playback. Verified via CDP against both real `AudioEngine` instances (core and Remix) after a full process restart: `context.sinkId` matched the Yeti device's real id on both, confirming actual playback audio routes there in dev mode. **Also caught and fixed as an immediate side effect of this same incident**: the app was mid-relaunch playback at the time this was reported, from `v0.1.24`'s resume-on-launch firing through the speakers on a routine dev restart — paused immediately via the running app's own global Play/Pause, not a code change, but worth noting since it's exactly the kind of unattended noise this whole fix exists to prevent going forward.

**Number-field scroll step size is now content-aware** (`numberBoxStepper.js`) — direct follow-up to the hover-scroll change above: "When i scroll for a unit on the q box it is a LOT so this should be adjusting depending on what i'm messing with." The base "one step" unit is now read from each input's own HTML `step` attribute (EQ Freq is `1`, EQ Gain/Q are `0.1`, scatter gap fields are `1`) instead of a single hardcoded `1` for every field — a plain scroll on Q (whole range 0.1–10) used to move nearly a third of its range in one tick. Every modifier tier now scales off that same base rather than its own separate constant: Ctrl is a tenth of the base step, Shift jumps to the next multiple of 10× the base, Alt to the next multiple of 100× — so Freq (base 1) keeps its exact original behavior (documented worked examples: 3→Shift→10, 13→Alt→100, both still hold once you're not below Freq's own 20Hz floor) while Gain/Q (base 0.1) get proportionally finer, sensible jumps instead of the old fixed multiples of 10/100 that were nonsensical against Q's 0.1–10 range. Verified via CDP against the real EQ Q and Freq fields: Q from 1 stepped to 1.1 (plain)/1.01 (Ctrl)/2 (Shift)/10 (Alt), all exactly matching the field's own 0.1 step; Freq's original documented behavior re-confirmed unchanged (25→26 plain, 23→30 Shift, 130→200 Alt).

(A/B Compare on the parametric EQ was also reported as "not working" via the same inbox batch, but investigation found it fully functional — data isolation between slots, live audio nodes, and UI fields all verified correct via CDP. The user confirmed after re-checking: they'd just missed that both slots start seeded with a copy of the same bands, so nothing looked different until one side was actually edited. No code change.)

**EQ bands gained a Gentle/Steep slope control** — the last item from the FL Studio parametric EQ clone's own research backlog (feasibility confirmed weeks earlier: ffmpeg's `highpass`/`lowpass` have a real `poles` parameter but `bandpass`/`bandreject` don't, and `BiquadFilterNode` has no slope parameter at all — cascading N identical filter instances in series was verified, on both sides, to be the one technique that works consistently across all four filter-*shaped* types). Only `lowpass`/`highpass`/`bandpass`/`notch` have a real slope concept (`SLOPE_CAPABLE_EQ_TYPES`, duplicated per the usual cross-file/cross-directory pattern in `EqEditor.js`, `SoundSource.js`, `PreviewSource.js`, and `loopClip.js`) — peaking/shelf bands are gain-shaped, not rolloff-shaped, and `'off'` does nothing, so the new Slope `<select>` next to Type hides/disables itself for those. `'steep'` means 4 identical cascaded stages (`STEEP_STAGE_COUNT`) instead of 1, each stage multiplying the rolloff by another ~12dB/octave (verified directly against the real bundled ffmpeg binary: four cascaded `highpass=f=1000:poles=2` instances measured ~34dB more attenuation than one).

**This meant a real architectural change**, similar in shape to the dynamic-node rework above but one level deeper: a "band" used to map to exactly one live `BiquadFilterNode`; now it maps to a *group* of 1–4 identical nodes chained internally. `SoundSource.js`/`PreviewSource.js` both gained an `eqNodeGroups` array (nodes re-grouped per band, for per-band parameter updates) alongside the existing flat `eqNodes` (still used for connect/disconnect bookkeeping), and their rebuild trigger changed from a plain band-count comparison to a per-band *topology fingerprint* (`eq.map(eqBandStageCount).join(',')`) — a plain length check can't tell a same-length type/slope change (which alters one band's own stage count) apart from a genuine no-op. `loopClip.js`'s `buildFilterChain` cascades the same single-band ffmpeg filter string `eqBandStageCount(band)` times in the `-af` chain rather than teaching `buildEqBandFilter` itself about repetition, keeping that function's per-type mapping unchanged. The EQ graph's composite response curve (`computeResponseCurve`) also folds stage count in — a steep band's own `getFrequencyResponse()` magnitude is summed in dB `stages` times rather than once, so the drawn curve honestly reflects the real cascaded rolloff, not just a single stage's shape.

**Real bug caught during verification, not code review**: `index.js`'s `snapshotOf()` (the Save button's dirty-check, used to enable/disable Save and to compute what "saved" means) rebuilds each EQ band from an explicit field list (`{freqHz, gainDb, q, type, muted}`) rather than spreading the whole band object — written before `slope` existed, so it silently dropped the new field from both sides of the comparison. Toggling slope updated the live audio, the graph, and the response curve correctly, but the Save button stayed disabled and labeled "Nothing to save" the entire time — a slope-only edit could never actually be saved. Caught by directly inspecting `hasUnsavedChanges()`'s two compared snapshot strings via CDP after seeing an unexpectedly-disabled Save button, not by assuming the change worked because everything else did. Fixed by adding `slope: b.slope ?? 'gentle'` to the same explicit field list.

**Verified via CDP end-to-end, both playback paths independently**: Remix's live preview correctly rebuilt a band's node group from 1→4 nodes on switching to Steep (and the Alt+click full reset correctly returns it to 1, resetting slope to `'gentle'` and hiding the control once type goes back to `'peaking'`); the *real* cascading effect was confirmed against the actual browser `BiquadFilterNode.getFrequencyResponse()` implementation (a notch band's off-center attenuation was exactly 4× larger in dB terms with 4 cascaded stages vs. 1, matching the math `stages × 20×log10(mag)` predicts); saving with a steep band produced a real, non-erroring ffmpeg bake and the persisted `filters.eq` correctly round-tripped `slope: 'steep'` through a full reload; and the identical mechanism was independently re-verified against core's `LocalFileSoundSource` in the actual Mixer (not just the Remix preview) by pushing a steep-slope filter update through the real `library.updateFilters` IPC call and confirming the live source's `eqNodeGroups` rebuilt to 4 nodes.

**App-wide scrollbars are now thin and low-opacity by default, brighter on hover** (`main.css`) — reported via inbox: "The app is really crowded with scrolling bars... making them disappear when not hovered or just removing them or maybe using a low opacity for it is better." A single unscoped `::-webkit-scrollbar`/`-track`/`-thumb`/`-thumb:hover` rule set (Chromium-only, which is all Electron ever renders with, so no fallback needed) applies to every scrollable element in the document — the shared `main` container every tab's panel scrolls inside, the Remix sound picker's own dropdown list, any modal's overflow — without needing a per-plugin copy, since it cascades globally regardless of which stylesheet the actual scrolling element's markup came from. Purely visual (`width`/`background`/`border-radius` on the thumb, transparent track), doesn't touch any scroll behavior. Verified visually via a real CDP screenshot of the Mixer's sound list (17 rows, genuinely overflowing `main`'s viewport) showing a thin, subtle scrollbar rather than the previous default full-width one.

**Tags gained Obsidian-style autocomplete** (`SoundRow.js`) — direct feedback, reusing the Remix sound picker's own proven dropdown shape (filtered list, arrow-key nav, mousedown-not-click on options so a selection doesn't first trigger the input's blur-commit). `allTags` (every tag used anywhere in the *full* library, deduped) is computed once per `render()` in `tabs/mixer/index.js` and threaded down through `renderSoundList`/`buildRow`/`createSoundRow` to each row's own tag input - deliberately sourced from the full library, not the possibly search/tag-filtered `entries` list, so autocomplete stays complete regardless of what's currently visible. Autocomplete operates on just the *current* (last) comma-segment being typed, so multi-tag comma entry keeps working unchanged; selecting a suggestion fills in its exact stored casing and arms the input for the next tag. On commit (blur/Enter with nothing highlighted), every typed segment is case-insensitively matched against `allTags` and normalized to the existing tag's own casing if one matches ("rain" → "Rain Sounds" if that's how it was first capitalized) - closing the loop on "when you hit enter it formats the tag the same way." Verified via CDP: typing a partial tag showed the right filtered suggestions; clicking a suggestion applied correct casing; manually typing a differently-cased existing tag still normalized correctly on blur; a genuinely new tag stayed as typed; arrow-down + Enter completed a highlighted suggestion without committing the whole field; Escape cancelled cleanly with nothing added.

**A/B Compare reworked from "two mutable comparison slots" into "current session (A) vs. last-saved-before-this-session (B, immutable)"** — direct feedback, with one round of clarification on wording. `EqEditorController` gained `setReadOnly(bool)`/`isReadOnly()`, gating every mutating interaction (drag, wheel-Q, double/Alt-click, right-click type-cycle, add/remove band, precision-field edits via `setSelectedBand`) behind a no-op while true — verified via CDP by attempting a direct mutation call while read-only and confirming nothing changed, not just that the UI buttons looked disabled. `index.js`'s `savedEqSnapshot` is captured once in `loadSound()` from the just-loaded entry's own data and deliberately never touched again for the rest of the session, not even by an in-session Save ("last saved... before the current session") — confirmed via CDP: saved mid-session, switched to B, and it still showed the pre-session value; only a fresh reload picks up the new saved state as the new B baseline. Switching to B stashes A's live bands (`eqLiveBandsStash`) so switching back resumes editing exactly where it left off, with nothing lost. A new full-width banner above the graph ("Click to revert to previous session's mix" — wording clarified directly after an initial vaguer draft) shows only while viewing A and only when A actually differs from B, and reverts by copying B onto A. **Real correctness gap caught during design, not verification**: `updateSaveButtonState()`'s existing dirty-check reads `currentFilters()`, which would read B's own read-only data as "the current state" while B is on screen (A's real edits are stashed, not loaded) - would have shown a false "Nothing to save" while genuinely-unsaved A edits sat hidden in the stash. Fixed by forcing Save disabled with an explicit "Viewing saved mix" label whenever B is being viewed, with a matching defensive early-return in `save()` itself.

**EQ node Q value ceiling raised from 10 to 20** — reported directly ("Feels like nodes Q value cap at 10 is too little"). Checked the FL Studio "Fruity Parametric EQ 2" reference first rather than just picking a bigger number: its own Q/bandwidth control isn't the same shape at all (a normalized 0-1 knob through its own internal curve, not a plain numeric Q), so there's no literal number to copy from it. Settled on 20 as a reasoned ceiling instead, matching common real-world EQ Q caps (FabFilter Pro-Q, Waves). Neither `BiquadFilterNode.Q` nor ffmpeg's `width_type=q` have a hardcoded max, so this is a pure UI-constant change (`Q_MAX` in `EqEditor.js` + the field's `max` attribute). Verified via CDP: Q=15 now commits instead of clamping to 10, and the clamp still correctly caps at the new ceiling (25 → 20).

**Right-click an EQ node to cycle its type, plus a per-type color, initials, and a hover-bloom glow** (`EqEditor.js`) — requested directly, plus a hover-bloom addition made straight to `Board.md`. Right-click cycles `band.type` through the same order as the Type `<select>`, wrapping around; self-contained in the controller (mutate + `onChange`, no external callback needed, unlike Alt+click's reset which defers "what does reset mean" to `index.js`). Node fill now encodes type (`TYPE_COLORS`, 7 colors + `off` staying the plain dimmed gray) instead of selection state; selection is now a bigger radius + pink ring instead. A 1-2 letter initial (`TYPE_INITIALS`) draws inside each node; radius bumped 6/8px → 9/11px to fit it. Hover bloom is a larger, `shadowBlur`-based translucent circle drawn behind the crisp node. Verified via CDP: dispatched real `contextmenu` events at a node's computed canvas position, confirmed the type cycled correctly (`peaking→off→lowpass→highpass→bandpass`) and Save went dirty; a real hover+screenshot confirmed the green "BP" node, its glow, and the dimmed muted node beside it all rendered correctly.

**Remix's Save button moved into the sticky bar** — reported directly: "It's annoying just changing something on the top of the tab and then having to scroll right to the end just to save." Relocated from the bottom of the scrollable panel (`.editor-actions`) into `mountSticky()`, alongside Play/Pause/Loop/Volume, so it's reachable regardless of scroll position - the button, its icon, and its dynamic label (`Save`/`Saving…`/`Nothing to save`) are unchanged, only *where* they're wired from moved (`this.els.save`/`saveLabel` now populated by `mountSticky()` instead of `mount()`, click listener moved to match). The longer save-status sentence (baked/failed/skipped) deliberately stayed in the panel body - a glance at the sticky label already gives the state that matters most, the fuller confirmation sentence doesn't need to fit in a compact single-row bar. `.tab-sticky-panel` (core, `main.css`) gained `flex-wrap: wrap` as a defensive measure matching the toolbar's own earlier overflow fix, verified via a real narrow-window screenshot showing the row wrapping cleanly to a second line rather than clipping. `.editor-sticky-save` deliberately avoids `flex-shrink: 0` - a past real regression (the icon+text pass, `v0.1.59`) already established that *this specific button* needs to stay free to shrink (its longest state, "Nothing to save", is meaningfully wider than "Save") rather than forcing a tight row to overflow; `min-width: 0` was used instead to make sure it actually can. The now-fully-unused `.spacer` utility class (only consumer was the old Save-button layout) was deleted rather than left dead. Verified via CDP: loaded a sound, edited a filter at the top of the panel, confirmed the sticky Save button (not the old bottom-of-panel one, which no longer exists) immediately reflected the dirty state with zero scrolling, clicked it, confirmed a real successful bake, and confirmed the button correctly returns to "Nothing to save" afterward.

**BUG FIX: Random Interval (scatter) playback silently stopped after its first shot for any sound not eligible for the baked-buffer path** — reported directly against the real installed app ("I'm trying like hell to add a sound as a randomized playing loop but it isn't working... The funny thing is i managed to add a sound as a randomized loop playing which was the wind chimes one. weird..."). Root cause, confirmed live against the real installed app's own `library.json` plus a reproducible dev-mode repro: `StreamScatterSource._watchForShotEnd()` (`src/renderer/audio/ScatterSoundSource.js`) detected a shot's end by recursively self-scheduling via `requestAnimationFrame` - which Chromium throttles to near-zero once the window is hidden or unfocused (confirmed directly: patching the global `requestAnimationFrame` to log every call showed exactly 1 call total over a 2.5-second window with the dev window minimized, `document.hidden === true` throughout). Noctívago's window is *expected* to be backgrounded during normal ambient-sound use (it's not a foreground app), so this silently killed the entire shot-rescheduling chain after the very first shot, forever, for any scatter sound using the stream fallback - while `BufferScatterSource` (baked-clip path) kept working fine, since it detects shot-end via the native `AudioBufferSourceNode.onended` event instead, which isn't tied to the visual frame clock at all. This is the same class of bug for *any* stream-mode scatter sound, matching "mourning dove doesn't work, wind chimes does" if the two differed in buffer-mode eligibility. Fixed by replacing the rAF polling loop with listeners on the `<audio>` element's own native `timeupdate`/`pause`/`ended` events (`_onShotProgress`/`_watchForShotEnd`/`_stopWatchingShotEnd`) - these fire off the media element's own playback clock, which keeps running in the background (the same mechanism that lets background audio play at all), so shot-end detection now does too. Verified via CDP against a real reproduction: minimized the dev window via a real Win32 `ShowWindow`/`SW_MINIMIZE` call (not a faked `document.hidden` override - genuine OS-level backgrounding), confirmed `document.hidden === true` throughout, then sampled a scatter source's `audioEl.currentTime` every 250ms for 8 seconds and confirmed it correctly cycled through 4+ shots (resetting to ~0 roughly every 2.1s, matching the test clip's own duration) the entire time the window stayed hidden - proving the fix holds under the exact real-world condition that caused the original bug, not just in the foreground-focused state most of this session's other CDP tests ran in.

**Random Interval (scatter) shots gained ease in/out and per-shot volume randomization** — reported via the Obsidian inbox alongside the background-playback bug above ("There should be an ease in and ease out on sounds that are on the randomized interval playing. Also there should be more parameters to configure the randomizing"), with a direct answer to the inbox's own "should this be a new plugin?" question: no - scatter is already per-sound config inside the Remix plugin alongside trim/filters/speed-pitch, splitting it out would fragment one sound's settings across two disconnected surfaces for no benefit. New `entry.scatter` fields: `minVolume`/`maxVolume` (0-1 fraction, default 1/1 - unlike pitch, 0 isn't a neutral value for volume, so there's no "both 0 = fully random" special case here; a user who wants that can already just set min to 0 directly) and `fadeInMs`/`fadeOutMs` (default 0/0, today's existing hard on/off). `ScatterSoundSource.js` gained `randomVolumeScale()`, `fadeSeconds()` (clamps fade-in/fade-out to at most half the shot's own duration, same pattern `loopClip.js`'s crossfade clamp already uses, so they can't overlap/invert on a short shot), and a shared `scheduleShotEnvelope()` used by both source variants. **Both variants needed a new per-shot gain stage**: `BufferScatterSource` gets a fresh `shotGain` node per shot (alongside the existing fresh `AudioBufferSourceNode`), cleaned up in `onended`/`pause()`/`dispose()` alongside the node it pairs with; `StreamScatterSource` gets one persistent `shotGainNode` spliced into its chain (`filterGainNode → shotGainNode → gainNode`), reset at the start of every shot rather than recreated, since its `<audio>` element itself is long-lived across shots unlike the buffer variant's one-node-per-shot model. **Real correctness detail caught while implementing, not after**: `AudioBufferSourceNode.detune` isn't actually free of duration effects the way `v0.1.34`'s original comment claimed - per the Web Audio spec it combines into the same `computedPlaybackRate` a plain `.playbackRate` change would (`playbackRate × 2^(detune/1200)`), so a pitched-up shot genuinely finishes sooner in real time. Both source variants now compute each shot's *actual* pitch-adjusted duration (dividing the nominal duration by that combined rate) before scheduling the fade envelope, so fade-out correctly lands at the shot's real end regardless of what pitch it happened to roll - the old, incorrect "no effect on duration" claim in the file's own class-doc comment was corrected in the same pass. Remix's Playback Mode section gained Volume (min/max, range sliders 0-100%) and Fade in/Fade out (number fields, ms) alongside the existing Gap/Pitch controls - `snapshotOf()`'s explicit scatter-field list (the same spot a past slope-only-edit bug came from, see the Gentle/Steep entry above) was updated to include all four new fields from the start this time, verified by confirming Save correctly re-disabled to "Nothing to save" immediately after saving a fade/volume-only change, not staying stuck dirty. **Verified via CDP against the real dev app**: confirmed all four new fields render with correct neutral defaults (100%/100%, 0ms/0ms) on a fresh sound; set custom values (40%-90% volume, 500ms fade-in, 700ms fade-out), saved, and confirmed the exact values round-tripped through `library.json`; included the sound in the real Mixer and directly sampled the live `shotGain`/`shotGainNode` AudioParam value every 100ms across a full shot cycle, confirming it faded down to ~0, then back up to a new per-shot randomized target within the configured 0.4-0.9 range - including one sample run that happened to land a large random pitch shift, which correctly produced a shorter real shot duration and a correspondingly compressed (clamped) fade shape, confirming the pitch-duration coupling fix above is actually wired into the fade math, not just present in a comment.

**Solo button in Remix** — picked up via the standing ranked-backlog system after a direct request to "use the system to determine the order" (owner-mandated 0-10 effort + 0-10 time scoring, lowest average first; this scored 2.5, the lowest of everything open). Mutes every other already-playing Mixer sound while auditioning the one currently open in Remix, so it's audible against silence instead of the full mix. **Cross-plugin-boundary problem, not a simple toggle**: Remix has no direct access to core's module-scoped Mixer state (plugins only get the curated `window.noctivago` IPC surface), and mute is deliberately session-only/unpersisted (same as global mute), so there's no library data to round-trip through either. Bridged with a plain `window` `CustomEvent` (`noctivago:remix-solo`, `{soundId, active}`) - the simplest connection that doesn't require either side to import the other, matching how the Mixer already talks to the system tray. Core's `applyRemixSolo()` (`tabs/mixer/index.js`) mutes every id in `state.included` except the soloed one, but only ones not already muted - tracked separately in a new `state.soloMutedByRemix` Set so un-soloing only reverts what solo itself changed, never a sound the user had already muted by hand. Auto-un-solos on leaving the Remix tab (`onHide`) and when switching to a different sound within Remix (`loadSound()`, targeting the *previous* sound's id before `currentEntry` reassigns) - same "fresh load/leave = don't linger" pattern the EQ graph's own per-band Solo already established, so soloing can never silently leave other sounds muted after the fact. Lives in the sticky bar (`mountSticky`) alongside Play/Pause/Mute/Save, plain text toggle matching the EQ Solo button's own visual language (`.editor-eq-toggle-btn`-style, new `.editor-sticky-solo`/`-active` classes since the sticky bar's icon-only buttons don't fit a text-labeled toggle). **Verified via CDP against the real dev app**: soloing correctly muted a second Mixer sound's live gain to 0 (both `state.mutedSounds` and the actual source's `AudioParam`) while leaving the soloed sound itself untouched; un-soloing via the button restored the muted sound's exact real volume; switching tabs away from Remix while still soloed correctly auto-restored it without needing the button; and the key non-destructive case - manually muting a sound *before* soloing - correctly left it muted after a full solo/un-solo cycle (tracked as already-muted, never added to `soloMutedByRemix`, so un-solo never touched it).

**Solo, corrected to be Mixer-scoped** — direct correction right after the above shipped: "this solo button is cool and all but what i meant is i wanted a solo button for the mixer tab. So you can single one audio out vs the multiple that are playing." The original inbox note ("solo button for the sounds on the remix tab") was ambiguous about *where* the button should live; the Remix-scoped version above answered a real but different question (audition the sound open in Remix against the rest of the mix) than the one actually asked (isolate any one sound directly from the Mixer's own list). Kept the Remix button - still genuinely useful - and added the actual ask alongside it: each Mixer sound row gets its own Solo button (`SoundRow.js`, next to Mute, a plain "S" badge - no single universal solo icon the way mute/speaker already has one, and DAWs converge on the same letter convention). **Single-target solo** (soloing a second sound switches the target rather than stacking, matching how DAW solo buttons behave) - `tabs/mixer/index.js`'s `applyRemixSolo` was generalized into `applySolo(id, active)` + a shared `releaseSolo()`, with `state.soloedSoundId` now tracking the current target explicitly. **Real correctness gap caught and fixed during the generalization, not after**: switching the solo target directly from one entry point to the other (solo sound A via a Mixer row, then solo sound B via Remix's own button without un-soloing A first) would have left B - the *new* target - stuck muted, since the old code only ever excluded "the id passed as soloId" from its mute pass and never accounted for that id already being muted by a *different*, still-active solo. Fixed by having `applySolo(id, true)` release any other active target first, whenever one exists, before establishing the new one - makes it safe to call from either entry point at any time regardless of what's currently soloed. The window event bridge was renamed `noctivago:remix-solo` → `noctivago:solo` to match (now shared by both entry points, not Remix-specific). **Verified via CDP against the real dev app**: soloing a Mixer row correctly muted the other two included sounds' live gain and left the soloed one alone; switching the solo target to a different row correctly restored the first and muted the new set instead; the cross-entry-point handoff (solo via a Mixer row, then solo a *different* sound via Remix) correctly unmuted the new Remix-side target - the exact case that would have failed pre-fix; leaving Remix while soloed still auto-released everything; and pre-muting a sound by hand, then running a full solo/un-solo cycle on a different sound, correctly left the hand-muted one untouched throughout.

**Remix EQ graph: the live spectrum no longer shifts as you drag bands, and a new overlay shows what your current bands would actually cut** — picked up via the ranked backlog system (scored 3, lowest open item at the time), reported directly: "Spectogram on the remix tab shouldn't change when you mess around with the bands. This creates a false sense that you're not cutting out the frequencies... the frequencies outside of the allowed section should have an overlay to indicate that they're going to be completely cut." Root cause of the first half: the spectrum's analyser tap sat at `crossfadeGain`, *after* the whole EQ chain, so it visibly reshaped as a band was dragged - hard to judge "how much of the original signal is there" against a moving target. Retapped from `lowpassNode` instead (`PreviewSource.js`) - after the app's other two filter controls (highpass/lowpass, not excluded, since the ask was specifically about the parametric bands) but *before* the EQ chain, so it's now a stable reference. **Real wiring subtlety**: `rebuildEqChain`'s own `lowpassNode.disconnect()` (needed whenever the EQ topology changes, to rewire the band chain) would silently sever this tap too, since `.disconnect()` with no arguments clears every outgoing connection from a node - fixed by re-connecting the tap at the end of every `applyFiltersToVoice` call rather than just once at voice creation (a no-op when already connected, so this is safe regardless of whether a rebuild happened that call). Second half: a new `drawCutOverlay()` (`EqEditor.js`) shades every contiguous frequency range where the *already-computed* composite response curve drops at or below a -18dB threshold (a reasoned round number - deep enough to read as "cut" rather than "quieter," without waiting for the graph's own -24dB floor) - reuses the exact response-curve data the curve line itself is drawn from, so the two can never disagree. **Verified via CDP against the real dev app, at the pixel level, not just visually**: sampled actual canvas `getImageData` at a Y position above the spectrum fill's own reach, confirming the overlay's exact color appears only past the real computed cutoff boundary and nowhere else; then dragged a steep lowpass band from 2000Hz down to 300Hz and re-sampled, confirming the overlay boundary shifted to match while pixels far from the change stayed byte-identical - direct proof the spectrum layer itself is decoupled from band position while the overlay correctly tracks it.

**"Test fire" button for Random Interval (scatter) sounds, in the Mixer** — picked via the ranked backlog system (first item scored under the new 3-dimension method, 4/4/2 = 3.3, lowest open). Reported directly: "an override play button on the mixer tab for audios that are played at random intervals... play the audio once when pressed so you can test the configuration that you set up without having to remove and re-add the audio." A play-triangle icon button on each scatter-mode sound's own row (`SoundRow.js`), regardless of whether it's currently included - the whole point is not needing to add it first. Two real cases, handled differently: a sound **already live in the mix** gets a new `fireNow()` on `BufferScatterSource`/`StreamScatterSource` that just cancels whatever gap is currently pending and plays a shot immediately - normal continuous cycling resumes on its own right after, unaffected. A sound **not currently included** builds a throwaway source (same buffer/stream eligibility check real playback uses, so the test accurately reflects what it'll actually sound like once added) with a new `oneShot: true` constructor option - plays exactly one shot, then calls `onOneShotComplete` (which just disposes it) instead of scheduling another, never touching `state.included`/`state.playing`/`state.sources` at any point, so there's nothing left to remove afterward. Always plays at the sound's real configured volume regardless of any lingering mute flag from a previous include, since a "let me hear it" action should always be audible. **Verified via CDP against the real dev app**: patched `AudioContext.createBufferSource` to confirm exactly one shot fires for the not-included case (and stays at exactly one even several seconds past the clip's own duration, confirming no reschedule); confirmed `state.included`/`state.sources` never gain an entry for that case; and for the already-included case, confirmed the shot fires at the exact moment of the click (not after the remaining gap) and that natural cycling resumes afterward (a further shot fired on its own once the next gap elapsed).

**Remix: "Save as copy"** — picked via the ranked backlog system after a tie-break question (two items scored 4.33, owner picked this one). Reported directly: "take for example a birds call audio that has multiple instances of the bird singing and cut them into different segments then save as a new audio on the Mixer tab." New `library.js`'s `duplicateSound(id, {name})`: copies every field from the source entry into a brand-new id, sharing the *same* `originalPath`/`storageMode`/`storedFileName` rather than duplicating the underlying audio file - never a problem, since this app never writes to a sound's original file regardless (every trim/filter is metadata + a derived clip cache keyed by id, so a fresh id gets its own fresh bake slot for free). `loopClip*` fields reset to "not yet baked." Remix's new `saveAsCopy()` mirrors `save()`'s own structure closely (same field reads, same `renderLoopClip` call, same `MAX_BUFFER_CLIP_SECONDS` cap) but targets the new id instead of `this.currentEntry.id`, and deliberately never touches `this.currentEntry`/`this.savedSnapshot` at all - the owner's own workflow is trim segment 1, Save as copy, adjust the trim to segment 2 *on the same open sound*, Save as copy again, so the sound staying open in Remix has to keep behaving exactly as if nothing happened. Auto-names the copy ("`<name> copy`", "`<name> copy 2`"...) against the current library to avoid two identical-looking rows, rather than prompting - keeps the feature to a single button click, no new modal. New sticky-bar button next to Save (duplicate-icon + "Save as copy" text), disabled only while viewing the read-only A/B Compare "B" slot, same as Save - but unlike Save, always enabled while viewing A regardless of dirty state, since saving a copy of the *current* trim under a new name is meaningful even with nothing unsaved. **Verified via CDP against the real dev app**: trimmed a test sound to 1 of its original 2 seconds, clicked Save as copy, confirmed the source entry's own persisted trim stayed at the full 2s (untouched) while the new copy correctly got 0-1s; confirmed the copy's `originalPath` exactly matches the source's (no file duplication) and its own baked clip file is almost exactly half the source's file size, proving the bake reflects the new trim and not a copy of the source's own clip; and confirmed Remix's sound picker still shows the *original* sound selected afterward, not the new copy.

**Scheduled (clock-based) playback — a third `playMode` alongside Loop and Random Interval** — picked via the ranked backlog system (tied lowest at 4.33 alongside Save as copy; this was the one deferred by the tie-break). A sound can now trigger on a real wall-clock schedule instead of looping continuously or firing on a random gap: either a list of specific times of day (`schedule.times`, `"HH:MM"` strings) or a fixed interval aligned to midnight (`schedule.intervalMinutes`), configured in Remix's Playback Mode section alongside the existing Loop/Random Interval radios. New `src/renderer/audio/ScheduledSoundSource.js` (`BufferScheduledSource`/`StreamScheduledSource`) mirrors `ScatterSoundSource.js`'s buffer/stream-fallback shape but with deterministic timing instead of randomized gaps - no pitch/volume randomization or fade envelope, since a schedule is about *when*, not the shot's own character. Core scheduling math (`secondsUntilNextTrigger`) handles both modes: `interval` computes time until the next `intervalMinutes` boundary since midnight; `times` finds the soonest upcoming `HH:MM` from the list, rolling to tomorrow if every listed time has already passed today. `play()` deliberately schedules the next real trigger rather than firing immediately on include (a scheduled sound waits for its actual moment, unlike scatter) - which meant the Mixer's "test fire" button (see above) needed a new `fireNow()` path that starts the envelope and fires immediately for the not-yet-playing ephemeral case, reusing the exact same gate/dispatch `testFireOneShot` (renamed from `testFireScatter`) already had for scatter sounds. Threaded through the same duplication points every other playMode-aware piece of logic already has: `effectiveCrossfadeSeconds`/`desiredSourceKind`/`loopClipEligible` across `library.js`, `tabs/mixer/index.js`, and `plugins/editor/index.js` (forces crossfade to 0, same reasoning as scatter - a one-shot trigger is never rotated/crossfaded against itself).

**Real bug caught during verification, not code review**: a scheduled sound that passed every visible eligibility check (`loopClipReady`, matching trim points, matching filters) and had a real baked clip sitting on disk still fell back to stream mode, with no error logged anywhere - traced to `library.js`'s own `effectiveCrossfadeSeconds` helper (the main-process gate behind `getLoopClipPathForId`, which the `sound://…?variant=clip` protocol handler calls to decide whether to serve the baked file or 404) never having been updated for the new playMode - it still only special-cased `'scatter'`, so a scheduled entry's real, unforced `crossfadeSeconds` (e.g. `0.2`) got compared against the clip's actually-baked `0`, failed the match, and the protocol handler silently 404'd a file that was genuinely present. This is the exact same class of bug as the earlier Gentle/Steep-slope-missing-from-`snapshotOf` miss - a value duplicated across multiple files for architectural reasons (plugins can't import outside their own directory; main and renderer are separate processes) missed one of its three required update sites. Fixed by adding `|| entry.playMode === 'scheduled'` to `library.js`'s copy, matching the other two. Caught by directly fetching the `?variant=clip` URL from the live renderer and reading the raw HTTP status (404) rather than trusting the higher-level "is this sound playing" observation, after a first genuinely-mistaken lead (checking `hasUnsavedChanges()` too early in the load sequence, which looked like a `savedSnapshot`-undefined bug but was actually a test-timing race, not a real defect) was ruled out by a clean retest. **Verified via CDP against the real dev app after the fix**: confirmed the same `?variant=clip` fetch now returns `200`; confirmed the live Mixer source's `kind` resolves to `scheduled-buffer` (not `-stream`) for the same sound; and — the actual, uncut real-world proof — patched `AudioContext.createBufferSource` and let a real 1-minute-interval schedule run past its own boundary unattended, confirming a shot fired at exactly the correct wall-clock second (`01:45:00.149` local against an expected `01:45:00` trigger).

**Remix trim waveform: rendering-only smoothing pass to reduce blockiness at high zoom** — next-lowest-scored ranked backlog item, following a research pass (per [[feedback_research_before_complex_tasks]]) into how DAWs/waveform libraries actually render at arbitrary zoom levels: real tools combine a multi-resolution peak pyramid (real finer detail fetched as you zoom) with smooth interpolated rendering rather than nearest-neighbor stepping. Only the second half was implemented here - the first (fetching finer-grained peaks from ffmpeg past the existing resolution) stays a separate, bigger backlog item, since it needs a new IPC round-trip + caching design, not a quick follow-on. `waveform.js`'s `drawWaveform` used to floor each pixel column's time to the nearest fixed-resolution `peaks` entry and draw it as an independent vertical line segment - at high zoom, many consecutive columns landed on the exact same entry, producing a visible staircase of flat blocky bars. Now linearly interpolates min/max between the two neighboring peak entries per column and connects every column into one continuous filled polygon (top edge = interpolated max, bottom edge = interpolated min, closed back around) instead of drawing disconnected segments - the same shape a professional DAW's waveform silhouette actually takes. Honestly cosmetic smoothing of the same coarse data, not new precision: past the peaks array's own resolution there's still no more *real* detail to show, it just no longer visually reads as discrete blocks while there isn't. Verified via CDP against the real dev app: loaded a real sound in Remix, zoomed in significantly via synthetic wheel events on the canvas, and confirmed the waveform now renders as a smooth filled band rather than the standalone-verified-cheap alternative (no crash, no visual artifacts, existing loop-region overlay/tick marks/playhead all still draw correctly on top).

**BUG FIX: an uncaught exception during waveform-peak extraction could crash-loop the entire app with an unstoppable "JavaScript error occurred in the main process" dialog** — found by the agent's own testing (a malformed IPC call, `getWaveformPeaks(id)` with no `targetWidth`), then confirmed to be a real architectural gap, not just a testing artifact, before fixing it. Root cause: `runFfmpeg.js`'s shared `runFfmpegPipe` (the reusable ffmpeg-stdout-streaming wrapper `waveformPeaks.js`, `bandEnergy.js`, and `loopClip.js` all build on) wired the caller's per-chunk callback directly into the raw Node stream's own `'data'` event (`child.stdout.on('data', onChunk)`) with no try/catch - a synchronous throw inside `onChunk` (here: `extractWaveformPeaks`'s bucket-accumulation loop indexing into a zero-length `peaks` array, since `Array.from({length: undefined})` silently produces `[]`) propagates straight up through Node's stream internals as an uncaught main-process exception, completely bypassing every caller's own try/catch around the awaited promise (the throw happens well after that executor already returned). Electron's default handler shows a blocking dialog for this - and since the ffmpeg child process is never killed when its stdout handler throws, it keeps streaming and re-triggering the identical exception on every subsequent chunk, producing exactly the "won't stop popping up no matter how many times I click OK" symptom reported live, mid-session. **Fixed at the shared wrapper**, benefiting all four call sites at once (the project's own stated reason `runFfmpeg.js` is kept generic/domain-agnostic rather than each feature rolling its own spawn logic): `onChunk` is now called inside its own try/catch, killing the child and rejecting the promise on error instead of letting the throw escape - converting a fatal, unrecoverable crash into a normal, already-expected rejection (`ipc.js`'s `audio:getWaveformPeaks` handler already had a try/catch around this exact call, written assuming failures would surface as rejections - it just turned out that assumption didn't hold for a synchronous throw inside a raw stream callback). Also added a defensive guard directly in `extractWaveformPeaks` (`waveformPeaks.js`) rejecting immediately if `targetWidth` isn't a positive integer, since this function is reached over IPC - a real trust boundary where a bad argument should fail cleanly rather than silently corrupt internal state. **Verified via CDP against the real dev app**: repeated the exact malformed call that previously crashed the app and confirmed it now returns `null` with the app still fully alive and responsive afterward; confirmed a correctly-shaped call still returns real peak data unchanged; confirmed Remix's waveform still loads and renders correctly (including the smoothing fix above) after a full dev-app restart.

**Remix trim waveform: real finer-grained detail past the original resolution, closing out the other half of the blocky-waveform item** — `waveformPeaks.js`'s `extractWaveformPeaks` gained optional `windowStart`/`windowEnd` params: when present, ffmpeg seeks (`-ss`/`-t` as *input* options, before `-i`, for a fast seek rather than decoding from the start of a long file every time) and decodes just that narrower time range instead of the whole file, so the same `targetWidth` bucket count now spans a much smaller span - genuinely finer real detail, not a resample of the same coarse array. `LoopEditor.js` gained a second `detailPeaks`/`detailPeaksStart`/`detailPeaksEnd` slot alongside the existing full-file `peaks` - `redraw()` uses the detail array only while the current viewport is fully covered by it, falling back to the base array the instant the view moves past its edges (so there's never a moment showing stale detail for the wrong range). `waveform.js`'s `drawWaveform` now takes the peaks array's own `peaksStart`/`peaksEnd` time range as parameters (defaulting to `[0, duration]` for the base array) instead of always assuming `peaks` spans the whole file, so the same draw code works unchanged for either array. `plugins/editor/index.js`'s `maybeFetchDetailPeaks` (wired off the same `onViewportChange` the scrollbar already listens to) triggers only once the current view's seconds-per-pixel drops below the base array's own seconds-per-bucket - i.e., only once the user has actually zoomed in past what the existing resolution can show - debounced 250ms so a zoom gesture (many wheel ticks in quick succession) fires one ffmpeg pass for the settled end state, not one per tick.
